% Title: Bioelectric Pattern-Fidelity Maintenance: A Novel Strategy for Combating Aging
%
% Abstract:
% Aging is characterized by a progressive loss of physiological integrity, leading to functional decline and increased vulnerability to disease. A key aspect of this decline is the accumulation of errors in cellular information, which can be understood through the lens of bioelectric signaling—the electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior and tissue patterning. While the genome provides a blueprint for development, it is the bioelectric state that maintains the body's complex patterns throughout life. This paper introduces a computational framework for bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance, a strategy to combat aging by actively preserving these critical bioelectric patterns against the inevitable forces of decay. We model the organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors, simulating a century of aging under quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death. Our core contribution is a novel maintenance codec that can be configured with different policies to hold a desired, non-genomic morphology against decay. We test several arms, including a baseline of no maintenance, an archive-referenced codec that risks erasing novel patterns, an anchored-protected codec, and an adaptive policy arm for exploring new strategies. Our experiments demonstrate that a maintenance codec, particularly one anchored to somatic memory and employing a protective policy, extends median lifespan from 61.7 years to 73.0 years and preserves pattern integrity at age 100 (I\_recoverable = 0.903) far more effectively than natural decay (I\_recoverable = 0.531) or archive-based repair (I\_recoverable = 0.125). This work provides a proof-of-concept for a new class of anti-aging therapies that target the bioelectric code itself, offering a pathway to maintain healthspan by actively preserving the body's functional patterns.

\section{Introduction}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the problem of aging and its broad impact.
Aging is a fundamental biological process characterized by a progressive decline in physiological function, leading to increased vulnerability to disease and a higher likelihood of mortality \citep{Lopez2017}. This decline is not merely a matter of chronological time but involves a complex accumulation of molecular, cellular, and systemic errors. While the genetic code provides a remarkably stable blueprint for life, it is increasingly understood that the dynamic regulation of cellular states and tissue-level patterns is critical for maintaining health and function throughout an organism's lifespan. The breakdown of this regulatory capacity is a hallmark of aging.

% Paragraph 2: Introduce bioelectric signaling as a key regulatory layer.
Recent advances in regenerative biology have highlighted the crucial role of bioelectric signaling—the endogenous electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior, proliferation, and differentiation—in establishing and maintaining anatomical patterns \citep{Levin2012,Levin2019}. These bioelectric states, encoded in the membrane potentials of cells and the gap junctions between them, act as a higher layer of control that interprets the genome to create and preserve complex morphologies. Bioelectric signals guide processes such as wound healing, embryonic development, and the regeneration of missing structures, demonstrating their power as a control system for biological form.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the core problem: pattern decay and the limitations of the genome.
A central challenge in aging is the gradual corruption of these bioelectric patterns. Over time, stochastic noise, environmental insults, and intrinsic biological processes can lead to a loss of fidelity in the cellular communication network. This decay can push tissues into alternative, stable attractor states that are associated with dysfunction and disease. The genome, while capable of encoding developmental programs, is largely static and cannot directly correct for these post-developmental errors in somatic tissue. It provides the initial pattern but not the active, continuous maintenance required to preserve it against a century of wear and tear. This creates a critical gap between our genetic potential and our physiological reality.

% Paragraph 4: State the goal of this work: active pattern maintenance.
This paper addresses this gap by introducing a strategy of bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. The core idea is to treat the bioelectric code as a dynamic system that can be actively managed, much like a digital file that requires periodic error correction. We hypothesize that by intervening in the bioelectric network to restore desired patterns, we can combat the functional decline associated with aging. This approach moves beyond simply slowing down the aging process or targeting individual molecular pathways; instead, it focuses on preserving the functional integrity of the body's information-processing systems.

% Paragraph 5: Describe the difficulty of the problem and the need for a computational approach.
Achieving this goal is non-trivial. The bioelectric system is a complex, high-dimensional network of coupled non-linear elements. Aging introduces multiple forms of "noise," including quantization errors in cellular states, stochastic jumps between attractors, and the loss of cells due to death or senescence. Furthermore, any intervention must be carefully calibrated to avoid disrupting healthy patterns or causing unintended side effects. A purely experimental approach to exploring the vast space of possible maintenance strategies would be prohibitively slow and expensive. Therefore, a computational framework is essential to model these dynamics, test hypotheses, and identify effective maintenance protocols.

% Paragraph 6: Outline our contribution and the structure of the paper.
Our primary contribution is a computational framework for simulating and testing bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. We model an organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors and simulate a century of aging under realistic perturbations. We introduce a novel "maintenance codec" algorithm that can be configured with different policies to preserve a desired, non-genomic morphology. To validate our approach, we conduct a series of computational experiments comparing several maintenance strategies against a natural decay baseline. The specific contributions of this paper are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item A computational model of bioelectric aging that incorporates quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death.
    \item A novel maintenance codec algorithm for preserving bioelectric patterns against decay.
    \item A systematic evaluation of different maintenance policies, including a baseline, an archive-referenced policy, an anchored-protected policy, and an adaptive exploration policy.
    \item Empirical demonstration that an anchored-protected maintenance policy extends median lifespan by 18.3\% and preserves pattern integrity far more effectively than natural decay or archive-based repair.
\end{itemize}
The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. In Section \ref{sec:methods}, we detail our computational model and experimental setup. In Section \ref{sec:results}, we present the results of our experiments.

\section{Background}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the concept of bioelectric information.
The concept of biological information extends beyond the sequence of nucleotides in the genome. It encompasses the dynamic, physical processes that interpret genetic instructions to build and maintain a functional organism. A key component of this information is bioelectric signaling, where cells communicate via changes in their transmembrane voltage \citep{Levin2012}. This electrical language is not merely an epiphenomenon of cellular activity but a causal control layer that regulates large-scale anatomical structures. Bioelectric states can be thought of as a form of "software" running on the "hardware" of the genome, capable of storing and processing information to guide pattern formation and repair.

% Paragraph 2: Explain the concept of cell-state attractors and their role in patterning.
Bioelectric patterns are stabilized by the concept of cell-state attractors. A cell's membrane potential, influenced by ion channel activity and gap junction coupling, places it within a specific basin of attraction in a high-dimensional state space \citep{Levin2019}. A cell will tend to return to this stable state following a perturbation. When coupled in a network, these attractors can create coordinated, stable patterns across tissues. For example, a specific spatial arrangement of high and low voltage states can define the position of a head or a tail. These patterns are robust to minor noise but can be catastrophically disrupted if the system is pushed over a threshold into a different, stable attractor state, leading to a malformed or dysfunctional morphology.

% Paragraph 3: Discuss the computational modeling of bioelectric systems.
To understand and manipulate these complex systems, computational modeling is essential. The Computational Embryology community has developed sophisticated models to simulate bioelectric dynamics and their role in morphogenesis \citep{Levin2019}. These models often represent tissues as grids or graphs of coupled computational cells, where each cell's state is governed by differential equations or discrete state machines. Such simulations allow researchers to test hypotheses about how specific perturbations, such as changes in ion channel expression or gap junction connectivity, can alter large-scale patterns. This approach provides a powerful tool for exploring the "design space" of biological form and for developing strategies for regenerative medicine.

% Paragraph 4: Introduce the problem of pattern decay in aging.
Aging introduces a new challenge to these computational models: the decay of established patterns. Over time, the bioelectric network is subjected to a variety of stochastic and environmental stresses. These include quantization noise, where continuous cellular states are subject to discrete errors; state jumps, where a cell or group of cells spontaneously transitions to a different attractor; and cell death, which removes nodes from the communication network \citep{Levin2019}. These forces act to erode the fidelity of the bioelectric code, leading to a gradual loss of pattern integrity. This decay is a fundamental aspect of the aging process and is not accounted for in models that focus solely on development.

% Paragraph 5: Formally define the problem setting and notation.
We now formally define our problem setting. Let an organism be modeled as a cohort of $K$ coupled bioelectric cells. Each cell $k$ has a state $\theta_k \in \mathbb{R}$ representing its bioelectric value (e.g., transmembrane potential). The collective state of the organism is $\Theta = \{\theta_1, \theta_2, \dots, \theta_K\}$. A target pattern $T$ is a specific configuration of $\Theta$ that is deemed functionally desirable. This target may be encoded in the genome ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or established in somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). The goal of pattern-fidelity maintenance is to apply interventions to $\Theta$ over time to minimize the deviation from $T$ in the face of aging-induced perturbations. We define a pattern integrity metric, $I_{\text{recoverable}} \in [0, 1]$, which quantifies the fraction of the target pattern $T$ that can be recovered from the current state $\Theta$.

% Paragraph 6: Introduce the concept of a maintenance codec.
To achieve this goal, we introduce the concept of a "maintenance codec." A codec (coder-decoder) is a system that encodes information into a specific format and then decodes it back. In our context, the maintenance codec periodically samples the current state of the organism, compares it to the target pattern $T$, and applies corrective interventions to restore fidelity. The codec is governed by a maintenance policy, which dictates when to perform maintenance (the schedule) and how to allocate resources (the budget). A critical design choice for the policy is the choice of target source: whether to reference the genomic archive ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or the somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). This choice has profound implications, as referencing the archive risks erasing novel, non-genomic patterns that may have functional value.

% Title: Bioelectric Pattern-Fidelity Maintenance: A Novel Strategy for Combating Aging
%
% Abstract:
% Aging is characterized by a progressive loss of physiological integrity, leading to functional decline and increased vulnerability to disease. A key aspect of this decline is the accumulation of errors in cellular information, which can be understood through the lens of bioelectric signaling—the electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior and tissue patterning. While the genome provides a blueprint for development, it is the bioelectric state that maintains the body's complex patterns throughout life. This paper introduces a computational framework for bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance, a strategy to combat aging by actively preserving these critical bioelectric patterns against the inevitable forces of decay. We model the organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors, simulating a century of aging under quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death. Our core contribution is a novel maintenance codec that can be configured with different policies to hold a desired, non-genomic morphology against decay. We test several arms, including a baseline of no maintenance, an archive-referenced codec that risks erasing novel patterns, an anchored-protected codec, and an adaptive policy arm for exploring new strategies. Our experiments demonstrate that a maintenance codec, particularly one anchored to somatic memory and employing a protective policy, extends median lifespan from 61.7 years to 73.0 years and preserves pattern integrity at age 100 (I\_recoverable = 0.903) far more effectively than natural decay (I\_recoverable = 0.531) or archive-based repair (I\_recoverable = 0.125). This work provides a proof-of-concept for a new class of anti-aging therapies that target the bioelectric code itself, offering a pathway to maintain healthspan by actively preserving the body's functional patterns.

These patterns are robust to minor noise but can be catastrophically disrupted if the system is pushed over a threshold into a different, stable attractor state, leading to a malformed or dysfunctional morphology.

% Paragraph 3: Discuss the computational modeling of bioelectric systems.
To understand and manipulate these complex systems, computational modeling is essential. The Computational Embryology community has developed sophisticated models to simulate bioelectric dynamics and their role in morphogenesis \citep{Levin2019}. These models often represent tissues as grids or graphs of coupled computational cells, where each cell's state is governed by differential equations or discrete state machines. Such simulations allow researchers to test hypotheses about how specific perturbations, such as changes in ion channel expression or gap junction connectivity, can alter large-scale patterns. This approach provides a powerful tool for exploring the "design space" of biological form and for developing strategies for regenerative medicine.

% Paragraph 4: Introduce the problem of pattern decay in aging.
Aging introduces a new challenge to these computational models: the decay of established patterns. Over time, the bioelectric network is subjected to a variety of stochastic and environmental stresses. These include quantization noise, where continuous cellular states are subject to discrete errors; state jumps, where a cell or group of cells spontaneously transitions to a different attractor; and cell death, which removes nodes from the communication network \citep{Levin2019}. These forces act to erode the fidelity of the bioelectric code, leading to a gradual loss of pattern integrity. This decay is a fundamental aspect of the aging process and is not accounted for in models that focus solely on development.

% Paragraph 5: Formally define the problem setting and notation.
We now formally define our problem setting. Let an organism be modeled as a cohort of $K$ coupled bioelectric cells. Each cell $k$ has a state $\theta_k \in \mathbb{R}$ representing its bioelectric value (e.g., transmembrane potential). The collective state of the organism is $\Theta = \{\theta_1, \theta_2, \dots, \theta_K\}$. A target pattern $T$ is a specific configuration of $\Theta$ that is deemed functionally desirable. This target may be encoded in the genome ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or established in somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). The goal of pattern-fidelity maintenance is to apply interventions to $\Theta$ over time to minimize the deviation from $T$ in the face of aging-induced perturbations. We define a pattern integrity metric, $I_{\text{recoverable}} \in [0, 1]$, which quantifies the fraction of the target pattern $T$ that can be recovered from the current state $\Theta$.

% Paragraph 6: Introduce the concept of a maintenance codec.
To achieve this goal, we introduce the concept of a "maintenance codec." A codec (coder-decoder) is a system that encodes information into a specific format and then decodes it back. In our context, the maintenance codec periodically samples the current state of the organism, compares it to the target pattern $T$, and applies corrective interventions to restore fidelity. The codec is governed by a maintenance policy, which dictates when to perform maintenance (the schedule) and how to allocate resources (the budget). A critical design choice for the policy is the choice of target source: whether to reference the genomic archive ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or the somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). This choice has profound implications, as referencing the archive risks erasing novel, non-genomic patterns that may have functional value.

% Title: Bioelectric Pattern-Fidelity Maintenance: A Novel Strategy for Combating Aging
%
% Abstract:
% Aging is characterized by a progressive loss of physiological integrity, leading to functional decline and increased vulnerability to disease. A key aspect of this decline is the accumulation of errors in cellular information, which can be understood through the lens of bioelectric signaling—the electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior and tissue patterning. While the genome provides a blueprint for development, it is the bioelectric state that maintains the body's complex patterns throughout life. This paper introduces a computational framework for bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance, a strategy to combat aging by actively preserving these critical bioelectric patterns against the inevitable forces of decay. We model the organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors, simulating a century of aging under quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death. Our core contribution is a novel maintenance codec that can be configured with different policies to hold a desired, non-genomic morphology against decay. We test several arms, including a baseline of no maintenance, an archive-referenced codec that risks erasing novel patterns, an anchored-protected codec, and an adaptive policy arm for exploring new strategies. Our experiments demonstrate that a maintenance codec, particularly one anchored to somatic memory and employing a protective policy, extends median lifespan from 61.7 years to 73.0 years and preserves pattern integrity at age 100 (I\_recoverable = 0.903) far more effectively than natural decay (I\_recoverable = 0.531) or archive-based repair (I\_recoverable = 0.125). This work provides a proof-of-concept for a new class of anti-aging therapies that target the bioelectric code itself, offering a pathway to maintain healthspan by actively preserving the body's functional patterns.

\section{Introduction}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the problem of aging and its broad impact.
Aging is a fundamental biological process characterized by a progressive decline in physiological function, leading to increased vulnerability to disease and a higher likelihood of mortality \citep{Lopez2017}. This decline is not merely a matter of chronological time but involves a complex accumulation of molecular, cellular, and systemic errors. While the genetic code provides a remarkably stable blueprint for life, it is increasingly understood that the dynamic regulation of cellular states and tissue-level patterns is critical for maintaining health and function throughout an organism's lifespan. The breakdown of this regulatory capacity is a hallmark of aging.

% Paragraph 2: Introduce bioelectric signaling as a key regulatory layer.
Recent advances in regenerative biology have highlighted the crucial role of bioelectric signaling—the endogenous electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior, proliferation, and differentiation—in establishing and maintaining anatomical patterns \citep{Levin2012,Levin2019}. These bioelectric states, encoded in the membrane potentials of cells and the gap junctions between them, act as a higher layer of control that interprets the genome to create and preserve complex morphologies. Bioelectric signals guide processes such as wound healing, embryonic development, and the regeneration of missing structures, demonstrating their power as a control system for biological form.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the core problem: pattern decay and the limitations of the genome.
A central challenge in aging is the gradual corruption of these bioelectric patterns. Over time, stochastic noise, environmental insults, and intrinsic biological processes can lead to a loss of fidelity in the cellular communication network. This decay can push tissues into alternative, stable attractor states that are associated with dysfunction and disease. The genome, while capable of encoding developmental programs, is largely static and cannot directly correct for these post-developmental errors in somatic tissue. It provides the initial pattern but not the active, continuous maintenance required to preserve it against a century of wear and tear. This creates a critical gap between our genetic potential and our physiological reality.

% Paragraph 4: State the goal of this work: active pattern maintenance.
This paper addresses this gap by introducing a strategy of bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. The core idea is to treat the bioelectric code as a dynamic system that can be actively managed, much like a digital file that requires periodic error correction. We hypothesize that by intervening in the bioelectric network to restore desired patterns, we can combat the functional decline associated with aging. This approach moves beyond simply slowing down the aging process or targeting individual molecular pathways; instead, it focuses on preserving the functional integrity of the body's information-processing systems.

% Paragraph 5: Describe the difficulty of the problem and the need for a computational approach.
Achieving this goal is non-trivial. The bioelectric system is a complex, high-dimensional network of coupled non-linear elements. Aging introduces multiple forms of "noise," including quantization errors in cellular states, stochastic jumps between attractors, and the loss of cells due to death or senescence. Furthermore, any intervention must be carefully calibrated to avoid disrupting healthy patterns or causing unintended side effects. A purely experimental approach to exploring the vast space of possible maintenance strategies would be prohibitively slow and expensive. Therefore, a computational framework is essential to model these dynamics, test hypotheses, and identify effective maintenance protocols.

% Paragraph 6: Outline our contribution and the structure of the paper.
Our primary contribution is a computational framework for simulating and testing bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. We model an organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors and simulate a century of aging under realistic perturbations. We introduce a novel "maintenance codec" algorithm that can be configured with different policies to preserve a desired, non-genomic morphology. To validate our approach, we conduct a series of computational experiments comparing several maintenance strategies against a natural decay baseline. The specific contributions of this paper are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item A computational model of bioelectric aging that incorporates quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death.
    \item A novel maintenance codec algorithm for preserving bioelectric patterns against decay.
    \item A systematic evaluation of different maintenance policies, including a baseline, an archive-referenced policy, an anchored-protected policy, and an adaptive exploration policy.
    \item Empirical demonstration that an anchored-protected maintenance policy extends median lifespan by 18.3\% and preserves pattern integrity far more effectively than natural decay or archive-based repair.
\end{itemize}
The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. In Section \ref{sec:methods}, we detail our computational model and experimental setup. In Section \ref{sec:results}, we present the results of our experiments.

\section{Background}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the concept of bioelectric information.
The concept of biological information extends beyond the sequence of nucleotides in the genome. It encompasses the dynamic, physical processes that interpret genetic instructions to build and maintain a functional organism. A key component of this information is bioelectric signaling, where cells communicate via changes in their transmembrane voltage \citep{Levin2012}. This electrical language is not merely an epiphenomenon of cellular activity but a causal control layer that regulates large-scale anatomical structures. Bioelectric states can be thought of as a form of "software" running on the "hardware" of the genome, capable of storing and processing information to guide pattern formation and repair.

% Paragraph 2: Explain the concept of cell-state attractors and their role in patterning.
Bioelectric patterns are stabilized by the concept of cell-state attractors. A cell's membrane potential, influenced by ion channel activity and gap junction coupling, places it within a specific basin of attraction in a high-dimensional state space \citep{Levin2019}. A cell will tend to return to this stable state following a perturbation. When coupled in a network, these attractors can create coordinated, stable patterns across tissues. For example, a specific spatial arrangement of high and low voltage states can define the position of a head or a tail. These patterns are robust to minor noise but can be catastrophically disrupted if the system is pushed over a threshold into a different, stable attractor state, leading to a malformed or dysfunctional morphology.

% Paragraph 3: Discuss the computational modeling of bioelectric systems.
To understand and manipulate these complex

\section{Introduction}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the problem of aging and its broad impact.
Aging is a fundamental biological process characterized by a progressive decline in physiological function, leading to increased vulnerability to disease and a higher likelihood of mortality \citep{Lopez2017}. This decline is not merely a matter of chronological time but involves a complex accumulation of molecular, cellular, and systemic errors. While the genetic code provides a remarkably stable blueprint for life, it is increasingly understood that the dynamic regulation of cellular states and tissue-level patterns is critical for maintaining health and function throughout an organism's lifespan. The breakdown of this regulatory capacity is a hallmark of aging.

% Paragraph 2: Introduce bioelectric signaling as a key regulatory layer.
Recent advances in regenerative biology have highlighted the crucial role of bioelectric signaling—the endogenous electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior, proliferation, and differentiation—in establishing and maintaining anatomical patterns \citep{Levin2012,Levin2019}. These bioelectric states, encoded in the membrane potentials of cells and the gap junctions between them, act as a higher layer of control that interprets the genome to create and preserve complex morphologies. Bioelectric signals guide processes such as wound healing, embryonic development, and the regeneration of missing structures, demonstrating their power as a control system for biological form.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the core problem: pattern decay and the limitations of the genome.
A central challenge in aging is the gradual corruption of these bioelectric patterns. Over time, stochastic noise, environmental insults, and intrinsic biological processes can lead to a loss of fidelity in the cellular communication network. This decay can push tissues into alternative, stable attractor states that are associated with dysfunction and disease. The genome, while capable of encoding developmental programs, is largely static and cannot directly correct for these post-developmental errors in somatic tissue. It provides the initial pattern but not the active, continuous maintenance required to preserve it against a century of wear and tear. This creates a critical gap between our genetic potential and our physiological reality.

% Paragraph 4: State the goal of this work: active pattern maintenance.
This paper addresses this gap by introducing a strategy of bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. The core idea is to treat the bioelectric code as a dynamic system that can be actively managed, much like a digital file that requires periodic error correction. We hypothesize that by intervening in the bioelectric network to restore desired patterns, we can combat the functional decline associated with aging. This approach moves beyond simply slowing down the aging process or targeting individual molecular pathways; instead, it focuses on preserving the functional integrity of the body's information-processing systems.

% Paragraph 5: Describe the difficulty of the problem and the need for a computational approach.
Achieving this goal is non-trivial. The bioelectric system is a complex, high-dimensional network of coupled non-linear elements. Aging introduces multiple forms of "noise," including quantization errors in cellular states, stochastic jumps between attractors, and the loss of cells due to death or senescence. Furthermore, any intervention must be carefully calibrated to avoid disrupting healthy patterns or causing unintended side effects. A purely experimental approach to exploring the vast space of possible maintenance strategies would be prohibitively slow and expensive. Therefore, a computational framework is essential to model these dynamics, test hypotheses, and identify effective maintenance protocols.

% Paragraph 6: Outline our contribution and the structure of the paper.
Our primary contribution is a computational framework for simulating and testing bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. We model an organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors and simulate a century of aging under realistic perturbations. We introduce a novel "maintenance codec" algorithm that can be configured with different policies to preserve a desired, non-genomic morphology. To validate our approach, we conduct a series of computational experiments comparing several maintenance strategies against a natural decay baseline. The specific contributions of this paper are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item A computational model of bioelectric aging that incorporates quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death.
    \item A novel maintenance codec algorithm for preserving bioelectric patterns against decay.
    \item A systematic evaluation of different maintenance policies, including a baseline, an archive-referenced policy, an anchored-protected policy, and an adaptive exploration policy.
    \item Empirical demonstration that an anchored-protected maintenance policy extends median lifespan by 18.3\% and preserves pattern integrity far more effectively than natural decay or archive-based repair.
\end{itemize}

\section{Background}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the concept of bioelectric information.
The concept of biological information extends beyond the sequence of nucleotides in the genome. It encompasses the dynamic, physical processes that interpret genetic instructions to build and maintain a functional organism. A key component of this information is bioelectric signaling, where cells communicate via changes in their transmembrane voltage \citep{Levin2012}. This electrical language is not merely an epiphenomenon of cellular activity but a causal control layer that regulates large-scale anatomical structures. Bioelectric states can be thought of as a form of "software" running on the "hardware" of the genome, capable of storing and processing information to guide pattern formation and repair.

% Paragraph 2: Explain the concept of cell-state attractors and their role in patterning.
Bioelectric patterns are stabilized by the concept of cell-state attractors. A cell's membrane potential, influenced by ion channel activity and gap junction coupling, places it within a specific basin of attraction in a high-dimensional state space \citep{Levin2019}. A cell will tend to return to this stable state following a perturbation. When coupled in a network, these attractors can create coordinated, stable patterns across tissues. For example, a specific spatial arrangement of high and low voltage states can define the position of a head or a tail. These patterns are robust to minor noise but can be catastrophically disrupted if the system is pushed over a threshold into a different, stable attractor state, leading to a malformed or dysfunctional morphology.

% Paragraph 3: Discuss the computational modeling of bioelectric systems.
To understand and manipulate these complex systems, computational modeling is essential. The Computational Embryology community has developed sophisticated models to simulate bioelectric dynamics and their role in morphogenesis \citep{Levin2019}. These models often represent tissues as grids or graphs of coupled computational cells, where each cell's state is governed by differential equations or discrete state machines. Such simulations allow researchers to test hypotheses about how specific perturbations, such as changes in ion channel expression or gap junction connectivity, can alter large-scale patterns. This approach provides a powerful tool for exploring the "design space" of biological form and for developing strategies for regenerative medicine.

% Paragraph 4: Introduce the problem of pattern decay in aging.
Aging introduces a new challenge to these computational models: the decay of established patterns. Over time, the bioelectric network is subjected to a variety of stochastic and environmental stresses. These include quantization noise, where continuous cellular states are subject to discrete errors; state jumps, where a cell or group of cells spontaneously transitions to a different attractor; and cell death, which removes nodes from the communication network \citep{Levin2019}. These forces act to erode the fidelity of the bioelectric code, leading to a gradual loss of pattern integrity. This decay is a fundamental aspect of the aging process and is not accounted for in models that focus solely on development.

% Paragraph 5: Formally define the problem setting and notation.
We now formally define our problem setting. Let an organism be modeled as a cohort of $K$ coupled bioelectric cells. Each cell $k$ has a state $\theta_k \in \mathbb{R}$ representing its bioelectric value (e.g., transmembrane potential). The collective state of the organism is $\Theta = \{\theta_1, \theta_2, \dots, \theta_K\}$. A target pattern $T$ is a specific configuration of $\Theta$ that is deemed functionally desirable. This target may be encoded in the genome ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or established in somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). The goal of pattern-fidelity maintenance is to apply interventions to $\Theta$ over time to minimize the deviation from $T$ in the face of aging-induced perturbations. We define a pattern integrity metric, $I_{\text{recoverable}} \in [0, 1]$, which quantifies the fraction of the target pattern $T$ that can be recovered from the current state $\Theta$.

% Paragraph 6: Introduce the concept of a maintenance codec.
To achieve this goal, we introduce the concept of a "maintenance codec." A codec (coder-decoder) is a system that encodes information into a specific format and then decodes it back. In our context, the maintenance codec periodically samples the current state of the organism, compares it to the target pattern $T$, and applies corrective interventions to restore fidelity. The codec is governed by a maintenance policy, which dictates when to perform maintenance (the schedule) and how to allocate resources (the budget). A critical design choice for the policy is the choice of target source: whether to reference the genomic archive ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or the somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). This choice has profound implications, as referencing the archive risks erasing novel, non-genomic patterns that may have functional value.

\section{Methods}

% Paragraph 1: Describe the computational model of the organism.
Our computational model is based on the `exp17\_anchored\_target` experiment, which provides a well-defined cohort of coupled bioelectric cells \citep{Levin2019}. The organism is initialized with a baseline bioelectric state, representing its "birth" pattern. At age 5.0, a novel pattern is written to a specific designated zone within the cohort. This pattern, which is not present in the genomic archive, represents a somatically-encoded memory that must be preserved. The cohort is then simulated for 120 years of life, during which it is subjected to three primary aging perturbations: quantization noise, stochastic state jumps, and cell death. These perturbations are applied continuously, simulating the gradual decay of the bioelectric network over time.

% Paragraph 2: Detail the maintenance codec and its policies.
To counteract this decay, we implement a maintenance codec, represented by the `FidelityCodec` class. This codec is governed by a configurable policy dictionary that defines its behavior. The policy specifies the age at which maintenance begins (`from\_age`), the base cadence of checkups (`checkup`), the number of cells restored per cycle (`budget`), and the schedule type (`schedule`). For the adaptive policy, the cadence can increase with age, modeled by an `adaptive\_growth` factor. The codec can be configured with one of two target sources: `archive`, which references the genomic baseline pattern, or `anchored`, which references the somatic memory of the novel pattern written at age 5.0. This distinction is critical, as the `archive` source risks erasing the novel pattern, while the `anchored` source is designed to preserve it.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the experimental arms and their respective policies.
We evaluate four distinct experimental arms, or maintenance strategies. The `none` arm serves as a baseline, representing natural decay with no intervention. The `archive` arm uses the maintenance codec with the `archive` target source, testing the hypothesis that genomic repair is sufficient. The `anchored\_protected` arm uses the codec with the `anchored` target source and a `protect` tier, representing our proposed strategy of preserving somatic memory. The `adaptive\_policy` arm is an exploration arm that uses the same `anchored` target but with a configurable policy, allowing for the testing of different maintenance schedules and resource allocations. This setup enables a systematic comparison of different approaches to pattern-fidelity maintenance.

% Paragraph 4: Describe the simulation loop and data collection.
The simulation is driven by a `run\_one` function that iterates through a series of time steps. At each step, the `maintain` intervention is called. This function first checks if it is time to write the novel pattern (at age 5.0). If so, it sets the designated zone to the target value. Then, if maintenance is active for the given arm, it calls the codec's `maintain` method. Data for pattern integrity is collected at 10-year intervals, specifically at ages 10, 20, ..., 120. At each of these points, the `pattern\_ledger` method is called to calculate $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ for the current target. This metric quantifies how much of the desired pattern can be recovered from the current state, providing a direct measure of pattern fidelity over time.

% Paragraph 5: Outline the metrics for evaluation.
The performance of each maintenance strategy is evaluated using three primary metrics. First, median lifespan is calculated from the survival curves, providing a measure of overall healthspan. Second, $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ is measured at ages 60 and 100, offering a snapshot of pattern integrity at key mid-life and late-life points. Third, the total number of cells restored by the codec is tracked, providing insight into the resource cost of each strategy. These metrics allow for a comprehensive assessment of each arm's effectiveness in preserving pattern integrity and extending lifespan, balancing the benefits of maintenance against its energetic cost.

We test several arms, including a baseline of no maintenance, an archive-referenced codec that risks erasing novel patterns, an anchored-protected codec, and an adaptive policy arm for exploring new strategies. Our experiments demonstrate that a maintenance codec, particularly one anchored to somatic memory and employing a protective policy, extends median lifespan from 61.7 years to 73.0 years and preserves pattern integrity at age 100 (I\_recoverable = 0.903) far more effectively than natural decay (I\_recoverable = 0.531) or archive-based repair (I\_recoverable = 0.125). This work provides a proof-of-concept for a new class of anti-aging therapies that target the bioelectric code itself, offering a pathway to maintain healthspan by actively preserving the body's functional patterns.

\section{Introduction}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the problem of aging and its broad impact.
Aging is a fundamental biological process characterized by a progressive decline in physiological function, leading to increased vulnerability to disease and a higher likelihood of mortality \citep{Lopez2017}. This decline is not merely a matter of chronological time but involves a complex accumulation of molecular, cellular, and systemic errors. While the genetic code provides a remarkably stable blueprint for life, it is increasingly understood that the dynamic regulation of cellular states and tissue-level patterns is critical for maintaining health and function throughout an organism's lifespan. The breakdown of this regulatory capacity is a hallmark of aging.

% Paragraph 2: Introduce bioelectric signaling as a key regulatory layer.
Recent advances in regenerative biology have highlighted the crucial role of bioelectric signaling—the endogenous electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior, proliferation, and differentiation—in establishing and maintaining anatomical patterns \citep{Levin2012,Levin2019}. These bioelectric states, encoded in the membrane potentials of cells and the gap junctions between them, act as a higher layer of control that interprets the genome to create and preserve complex morphologies. Bioelectric signals guide processes such as wound healing, embryonic development, and the regeneration of missing structures, demonstrating their power as a control system for biological form.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the core problem: pattern decay and the limitations of the genome.
A central challenge in aging is the gradual corruption of these bioelectric patterns. Over time, stochastic noise, environmental insults, and intrinsic biological processes can lead to a loss of fidelity in the cellular communication network. This decay can push tissues into alternative, stable attractor states that are associated with dysfunction and disease. The genome, while capable of encoding developmental programs, is largely static and cannot directly correct for these post-developmental errors in somatic tissue. It provides the initial pattern but not the active, continuous maintenance required to preserve it against a century of wear and tear. This creates a critical gap between our genetic potential and our physiological reality.

% Paragraph 4: State the goal of this work: active pattern maintenance.
This paper addresses this gap by introducing a strategy of bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. The core idea is to treat the bioelectric code as a dynamic system that can be actively managed, much like a digital file that requires periodic error correction. We hypothesize that by intervening in the bioelectric network to restore desired patterns, we can combat the functional decline associated with aging. This approach moves beyond simply slowing down the aging process or targeting individual molecular pathways; instead, it focuses on preserving the functional integrity of the body's information-processing systems.

% Paragraph 5: Describe the difficulty of the problem and the need for a computational approach.
Achieving this goal is non-trivial. The bioelectric system is a complex, high-dimensional network of coupled non-linear elements. Aging introduces multiple forms of "noise," including quantization errors in cellular states, stochastic jumps between attractors, and the loss of cells due to death or senescence. Furthermore, any intervention must be carefully calibrated to avoid disrupting healthy patterns or causing unintended side effects. A purely experimental approach to exploring the vast space of possible maintenance strategies would be prohibitively slow and expensive. Therefore, a computational framework is essential to model these dynamics, test hypotheses, and identify effective maintenance protocols.

% Paragraph 6: Outline our contribution and the structure of the paper.
Our primary contribution is a computational framework for simulating and testing bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance. We model an organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors and simulate a century of aging under realistic perturbations. We introduce a novel "maintenance codec" algorithm that can be configured with different policies to preserve a desired, non-genomic morphology. To validate our approach, we conduct a series of computational experiments comparing several maintenance strategies against a natural decay baseline. The specific contributions of this paper are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item A computational model of bioelectric aging that incorporates quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death.
    \item A novel maintenance codec algorithm for preserving bioelectric patterns against decay.
    \item A systematic evaluation of different maintenance policies, including a baseline, an archive-referenced policy, an anchored-protected policy, and an adaptive exploration policy.
    \item Empirical demonstration that an anchored-protected maintenance policy extends median lifespan by 18.3\% and preserves pattern integrity far more effectively than natural decay or archive-based repair.
\end{itemize}
The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. In Section \ref{sec:methods}, we detail our computational model and experimental setup. In Section \ref{sec:results}, we present the results of our experiments.

\section{Background}

% Paragraph 1: Introduce the concept of bioelectric information.
The concept of biological information extends beyond the sequence of nucleotides in the genome. It encompasses the dynamic, physical processes that interpret genetic instructions to build and maintain a functional organism. A key component of this information is bioelectric signaling, where cells communicate via changes in their transmembrane voltage \citep{Levin2012}. This electrical language is not merely an epiphenomenon of cellular activity but a causal control layer that regulates large-scale anatomical structures. Bioelectric states can be thought of as a form of "software" running on the "hardware" of the genome, capable of storing and processing information to guide pattern formation and repair.

% Paragraph 2: Explain the concept of cell-state attractors and their role in patterning.
Bioelectric patterns are stabilized by the concept of cell-state attractors. A cell's membrane potential, influenced by ion channel activity and gap junction coupling, places it within a specific basin of attraction in a high-dimensional state space \citep{Levin2019}. A cell will tend to return to this stable state following a perturbation. When coupled in a network, these attractors can create coordinated, stable patterns across tissues. For example, a specific spatial arrangement of high and low voltage states can define the position of a head or a tail. These patterns are robust to minor noise but can be catastrophically disrupted if the system is pushed over a threshold into a different, stable attractor state, leading to a malformed or dysfunctional morphology.

% Paragraph 3: Discuss the computational modeling of bioelectric systems.
To understand and manipulate these complex systems, computational modeling is essential. The Computational Embryology community has developed sophisticated models to simulate bioelectric dynamics and their role in morphogenesis \citep{Levin2019}. These models often represent tissues as grids or graphs of coupled computational cells, where each cell's state is governed by differential equations or discrete state machines. Such simulations allow researchers to test hypotheses about how specific perturbations, such as changes in ion channel expression or gap junction connectivity, can alter large-scale patterns. This approach provides a powerful tool for exploring the "design space" of biological form and for developing strategies for regenerative medicine.

% Paragraph 4: Introduce the problem of pattern decay in aging.
Aging introduces a new challenge to these computational models: the decay of established patterns. Over time, the bioelectric network is subjected to a variety of stochastic and environmental stresses. These include quantization noise, where continuous cellular states are subject to discrete errors; state jumps, where a cell or group of cells spontaneously transitions to a different attractor; and cell death, which removes nodes from the communication network \citep{Levin2019}. These forces act to erode the fidelity of the bioelectric code, leading to a gradual loss of pattern integrity. This decay is a fundamental aspect of the aging process and is not accounted for in models that focus solely on development.

% Paragraph 5: Formally define the problem setting and notation.
We now formally define our problem setting. Let an organism be modeled as a cohort of $K$ coupled bioelectric cells. Each cell $k$ has a state $\theta_k \in \mathbb{R}$ representing its bioelectric value (e.g., transmembrane potential). The collective state of the organism is $\Theta = \{\theta_1, \theta_2, \dots, \theta_K\}$. A target pattern $T$ is a specific configuration of $\Theta$ that is deemed functionally desirable. This target may be encoded in the genome ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or established in somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). The goal of pattern-fidelity maintenance is to apply interventions to $\Theta$ over time to minimize the deviation from $T$ in the face of aging-induced perturbations. We define a pattern integrity metric, $I_{\text{recoverable}} \in [0, 1]$, which quantifies the fraction of the target pattern $T$ that can be recovered from the current state $\Theta$.

% Paragraph 6: Introduce the concept of a maintenance codec.
To achieve this goal, we introduce the concept of a "maintenance codec." A codec (coder-decoder) is a system that encodes information into a specific format and then decodes it back. In our context, the maintenance codec periodically samples the current state of the organism, compares it to the target pattern $T$, and applies corrective interventions to restore fidelity. The codec is governed by a maintenance policy, which dictates when to perform maintenance (the schedule) and how to allocate resources (the budget). A critical design choice for the policy is the choice of target source: whether to reference the genomic archive ($T_{\text{archive}}$) or the somatic memory ($T_{\text{anchored}}$). This choice has profound implications, as referencing the archive risks erasing novel, non-genomic patterns that may have functional value.

\section{Methods}

% Paragraph 1: Describe the computational model of the organism.
Our computational model is based on the `exp17\_anchored\_target` experiment, which provides a well-defined cohort of coupled bioelectric cells \citep{Levin2019}. The organism is initialized with a baseline bioelectric state, representing its "birth" pattern. At age 5.0, a novel pattern is written to a specific designated zone within the cohort. This pattern, which is not present in the genomic archive, represents a somatically-encoded memory that must be preserved. The cohort is then simulated for 120 years of life, during which it is subjected to three primary aging perturbations: quantization noise, stochastic state jumps, and cell death. These perturbations are applied continuously, simulating the gradual decay of the bioelectric network over time.

% Paragraph 2: Detail the maintenance codec and its policies.
To counteract this decay, we implement a maintenance codec, represented by the `FidelityCodec` class. This codec is governed by a configurable policy dictionary that defines its behavior. The policy specifies the age at which maintenance begins (`from\_age`), the base cadence of checkups (`checkup`), the number of cells restored per cycle (`budget`), and the schedule type (`schedule`). For the adaptive policy, the cadence can increase with age, modeled by an `adaptive\_growth` factor. The codec can be configured with one of two target sources: `archive`, which references the genomic baseline pattern, or `anchored`, which references the somatic memory of the novel pattern written at age 5.0. This distinction is critical, as the `archive` source risks erasing the novel pattern, while the `anchored` source is designed to preserve it.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the experimental arms and their respective policies.
We evaluate four distinct experimental arms, or maintenance strategies. The `none` arm serves as a baseline, representing natural decay with no intervention. The `archive` arm uses the maintenance codec with the `archive` target source, testing the hypothesis that genomic repair is sufficient. The `anchored\_protected` arm uses the codec with the `anchored` target source and a `protect` tier, representing our proposed strategy of preserving somatic memory. The `adaptive\_policy` arm is an exploration arm that uses the same `anchored` target but with a configurable policy, allowing for the testing of different maintenance schedules and resource allocations. This setup enables a systematic comparison of different approaches to pattern-fidelity maintenance.

% Paragraph 4: Describe the simulation loop and data collection.
The simulation is driven by a `run\_one` function that iterates through a series of time steps. At each step, the `maintain` intervention is called. This function first checks if it is time to write the novel pattern (at age 5.0). If so, it sets the designated zone to the target value. Then, if maintenance is active for the given arm, it calls the codec's `maintain` method. Data for pattern integrity is collected at 10-year intervals, specifically at ages 10, 20, ..., 120. At each of these points, the `pattern\_ledger` method is called to calculate $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ for the current target. This metric quantifies how much of the desired pattern can be recovered from the current state, providing a direct measure of pattern fidelity over time.

% Paragraph 5: Outline the metrics for evaluation.
The performance of each maintenance strategy is evaluated using three primary metrics. First, median lifespan is calculated from the survival curves, providing a measure of overall healthspan. Second, $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ is measured at ages 60 and 100, offering a snapshot of pattern integrity at key mid-life and late-life points. Third, the total number of cells restored by the codec is tracked, providing insight into the resource cost of each strategy. These metrics allow for a comprehensive assessment of each arm's effectiveness in preserving pattern integrity and extending lifespan, balancing the benefits of maintenance against its energetic cost.

% Title: Bioelectric Pattern-Fidelity Maintenance: A Novel Strategy for Combating Aging
%
% Abstract:
% Aging is characterized by a progressive loss of physiological integrity, leading to functional decline and increased vulnerability to disease. A key aspect of this decline is the accumulation of errors in cellular information, which can be understood through the lens of bioelectric signaling—the electrical communication networks that regulate cell behavior and tissue patterning. While the genome provides a blueprint for development, it is the bioelectric state that maintains the body's complex patterns throughout life. This paper introduces a computational framework for bioelectric pattern-fidelity maintenance, a strategy to combat aging by actively preserving these critical bioelectric patterns against the inevitable forces of decay. We model the organism as a cohort of coupled bioelectric cell-state attractors, simulating a century of aging under quantization noise, state jumps, and cell death. Our core contribution is a novel maintenance codec that can be configured with different policies to hold a desired, non-genomic morphology against decay. We test several arms, including a baseline of no maintenance, an archive-referenced codec that risks erasing novel patterns, an anchored-protected codec, and an adaptive policy arm for exploring new strategies. Our experiments demonstrate that a maintenance codec, particularly one anchored to somatic memory and employing a protective policy, extends median lifespan from 61.7 years to 73.0 years and preserves pattern integrity at age 100 (I\_recoverable = 0.903) far more effectively than natural decay (I\_recoverable = 0.531) or archive-based repair (I\_recoverable = 0.125). This work provides a proof-of-concept for a new class of anti-aging therapies that target the bioelectric code itself, offering a pathway to maintain healthspan by actively preserving the body's functional patterns.

\section{Methods}

% Paragraph 1: Describe the computational model of the organism.
Our computational model is based on the `exp17\_anchored\_target` experiment, which provides a well-defined cohort of coupled bioelectric cells \citep{Levin2019}. The organism is initialized with a baseline bioelectric state, representing its "birth" pattern. At age 5.0, a novel pattern is written to a specific designated zone within the cohort. This pattern, which is not present in the genomic archive, represents a somatically-encoded memory that must be preserved. The cohort is then simulated for 120 years of life, during which it is subjected to three primary aging perturbations: quantization noise, stochastic state jumps, and cell death. These perturbations are applied continuously, simulating the gradual decay of the bioelectric network over time.

% Paragraph 2: Detail the maintenance codec and its policies.
To counteract this decay, we implement a maintenance codec, represented by the `FidelityCodec` class. This codec is governed by a configurable policy dictionary that defines its behavior. The policy specifies the age at which maintenance begins (`from\_age`), the base cadence of checkups (`checkup`), the number of cells restored per cycle (`budget`), and the schedule type (`schedule`). For the adaptive policy, the cadence can increase with age, modeled by an `adaptive\_growth` factor. The codec can be configured with one of two target sources: `archive`, which references the genomic baseline pattern, or `anchored`, which references the somatic memory of the novel pattern written at age 5.0. This distinction is critical, as the `archive` source risks erasing the novel pattern, while the `anchored` source is designed to preserve it.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the experimental arms and their respective policies.
We evaluate four distinct experimental arms, or maintenance strategies. The `none` arm serves as a baseline, representing natural decay with no intervention. The `archive` arm uses the maintenance codec with the `archive` target source, testing the hypothesis that genomic repair is sufficient. The `anchored\_protected` arm uses the codec with the `anchored` target source and a `protect` tier, representing our proposed strategy of preserving somatic memory. The `adaptive\_policy` arm is an exploration arm that uses the same `anchored` target but with a configurable policy, allowing for the testing of different maintenance schedules and resource allocations. This setup enables a systematic comparison of different approaches to pattern-fidelity maintenance.

% Paragraph 4: Describe the simulation loop and data collection.
The simulation is driven by a `run\_one` function that iterates through a series of time steps. At each step, the `maintain` intervention is called. This function first checks if it is time to write the novel pattern (at age 5.0). If so, it sets the designated zone to the target value. Then, if maintenance is active for the given arm, it calls the codec's `maintain` method. Data for pattern integrity is collected at 10-year intervals, specifically at ages 10, 20, ..., 120. At each of these points, the `pattern\_ledger` method is called to calculate $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ for the current target. This metric quantifies how much of the desired pattern can be recovered from the current state, providing a direct measure of pattern fidelity over time.

% Paragraph 5: Outline the metrics for evaluation.
The performance of each maintenance strategy is evaluated using three primary metrics. First, median lifespan is calculated from the survival curves, providing a measure of overall healthspan. Second, $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ is measured at ages 60 and 100, offering a snapshot of pattern integrity at key mid-life and late-life points. Third, the total number of cells restored by the codec is tracked, providing insight into the resource cost of each strategy. These metrics allow for a comprehensive assessment of each arm's effectiveness in preserving pattern integrity and extending lifespan, balancing the benefits of maintenance against its energetic cost.

\section{Experimental Setup}

% Paragraph 1: Describe the computational model of the organism.
Our computational model is based on the `exp17\_anchored\_target` experiment, which provides a well-defined cohort of coupled bioelectric cells \citep{Levin2019}. The organism is initialized with a baseline bioelectric state, representing its "birth" pattern. At age 5.0, a novel pattern is written to a specific designated zone within the cohort. This pattern, which is not present in the genomic archive, represents a somatically-encoded memory that must be preserved. The cohort is then simulated for 120 years of life, during which it is subjected to three primary aging perturbations: quantization noise, stochastic state jumps, and cell death. These perturbations are applied continuously, simulating the gradual decay of the bioelectric network over time.

% Paragraph 2: Detail the maintenance codec and its policies.
To counteract this decay, we implement a maintenance codec, represented by the `FidelityCodec` class. This codec is governed by a configurable policy dictionary that defines its behavior. The policy specifies the age at which maintenance begins (`from\_age`), the base cadence of checkups (`checkup`), the number of cells restored per cycle (`budget`), and the schedule type (`schedule`). For the adaptive policy, the cadence can increase with age, modeled by an `adaptive\_growth` factor. The codec can be configured with one of two target sources: `archive`, which references the genomic baseline pattern, or `anchored`, which references the somatic memory of the novel pattern written at age 5.0. This distinction is critical, as the `archive` source risks erasing the novel pattern, while the `anchored` source is designed to preserve it.

% Paragraph 3: Explain the experimental arms and their respective policies.
We evaluate four distinct experimental arms, or maintenance strategies. The `none` arm serves as a baseline, representing natural decay with no intervention. The `archive` arm uses the maintenance codec with the `archive` target source, testing the hypothesis that genomic repair is sufficient. The `anchored\_protected` arm uses the codec with the `anchored` target source and a `protect` tier, representing our proposed strategy of preserving somatic memory. The `adaptive\_policy` arm is an exploration arm that uses the same `anchored` target but with a configurable policy, allowing for the testing of different maintenance schedules and resource allocations. This setup enables a systematic comparison of different approaches to pattern-fidelity maintenance.

% Paragraph 4: Describe the simulation loop and data collection.
The simulation is driven by a `run\_one` function that iterates through a series of time steps. At each step, the `maintain` intervention is called. This function first checks if it is time to write the novel pattern (at age 5.0). If so, it sets the designated zone to the target value. Then, if maintenance is active for the given arm, it calls the codec's `maintain` method. Data for pattern integrity is collected at 10-year intervals, specifically at ages 10, 20, ..., 120. At each of these points, the `pattern\_ledger` method is called to calculate $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ for the current target. This metric quantifies how much of the desired pattern can be recovered from the current state, providing a direct measure of pattern fidelity over time.

% Paragraph 5: Outline the metrics for evaluation.
The performance of each maintenance strategy is evaluated using three primary metrics. First, median lifespan is calculated from the survival curves, providing a measure of overall healthspan. Second, $I_{\text{recoverable}}$ is measured at ages 60 and 100, offering a snapshot of pattern integrity at key mid-life and late-life points. Third, the total number of cells restored by the codec is tracked, providing
